# Mipham's Commentary on Precious Qualities: Entering the True Intent of the Buddha's Mother ^0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^0-1]]

Herein lies the excellent explanation titled Commentary on Precious Qualities: Entering the True Intent of the Buddha's Mother. ^0-1

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^0-2]]

Explanation according to the directly taught method of emptiness. The meaning of the title. Herein lies the excellent explanation titled Commentary on Precious Qualities: Entering the True Intent of the Buddha's Mother. Om svasti. I pay respectful homage to the Victor, the Kinsman of the Sun, his heirs Maitreya and Mañjughoṣa, Nāgārjuna, and the rest—the assembly of spiritual friends who hold the lineage of the Victors and teach the profound middle way. With a mind of devotion to the supreme Mother, the source of the Victors, their heirs, and their disciples—the assembly of noble ones of the three times, as vast as space—I shall excellently express the method of entering her ways. Here, among all the inexhaustible treasuries of Dharma spoken by all the Victors of the three times, it is taught that the noble prajñāpāramitā is the very essence and the ultimate. Within that, although there are many variations, such as the widely known seventeen mothers and sons, in this Verse Summary of Precious Qualities the entire meaning of them all is completely gathered. Furthermore, because its metrical composition is clear, because it is the word spoken from the very mouth of the Buddha, and because by explaining and listening to this text—which is exalted by many special qualities—one explains and listens to the entirety of the prajñāpāramitā, therefore, with a pure intention and relying on the instructions of the holy ones, in expressing the way of entering into its true intent, ^0-2

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^0-3]]

there are two parts: the explanation according to the directly taught method of emptiness, and the explanation according to the implicitly taught clear realizations of the path. Of these two, ^0-3

## Explanation According to the Approach of Emptiness Explicitly Taught ^1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-1]]

For the first, there are four parts: the meaning of the title, the translator's homage, the meaning of the main text, and the meaning of the conclusion. ^1-1

### Meaning of the Title ^1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-2]]

First. In the Indian language 'ārya', which in the Tibetan language is 'noble'. Likewise, 'prajñā' is 'wisdom', 'pāramitā' is 'perfection', 'sañcaya' is 'summary', and 'gāthā' is 'verse'. The extensive meaning of the words of the title should be understood from the context of the main text. Briefly, it is called 'noble' because it is a profound supramundane Dharma. The wisdom that realizes suchness, the natural state of all phenomena, is supreme and most excellent among all kinds of wisdom, so it is called the 'perfection of wisdom'. Because it teaches by taking that very non-conceptual wakefulness as its subject matter, this text also receives the title of the perfection of wisdom. Because by relying on this text one gathers and retains all the precious qualities of the mundane and supramundane paths and fruitions, it is called a 'summary'. It is a 'verse', bound by the noble melody of the eleven-syllable indravajrā meter. ^1-2

### Translator's Homage ^1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-3]]

The translator's homage. Second, before the great translators of the past translated this profound Dharma teaching the ultimate into the Tibetan language, they placed the words 'Homage to the noble Mañjuśrī', their special deity, in order to be free from obstacles and to increase merit. The meaning of the main text. ^1-3

### Meaning of the Main Text ^1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-4]]

Third, regarding the meaning of the main text, there are two parts: the connecting verse of the introductory setting, and the actual meaning of the main text. The connecting verse of the introductory setting. ^1-4

#### Connecting Words of the Introduction ^1-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-5]]

First, in the excellent place, Vulture Peak Mountain; the excellent teacher, the King of Śākyas, Śākyasiṃha himself; amidst the excellent retinue, the vast gathered assembly of śrāvakas, bodhisattvas, and others; at the excellent time immediately following his completion of teaching the Hundred Thousand Mother of the Victors and so forth—'then again' the teacher, the 'Bhagavān' himself, 'in order to make' 'those four retinues' of monks and the rest 'perfectly and completely joyous' with the qualities of the path and fruition of the Great Vehicle by easily realizing the great meaning with little difficulty, 'again taught this' profound 'perfection of wisdom', the excellent Dharma to be expressed, and 'at that time spoke these verses' as the expressing words. ^1-5

#### Actual Meaning of the Main Text ^1-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-6]]

The actual meaning of the main text. Second, there are three parts: exhorting to listen to the Dharma, the nature of the Dharma being taught, and the conclusion of that. ^1-6

##### Exhortation to Listen to the Dharma ^1-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-7]]

Exhorting to listen to the Dharma. For the first, there are two parts: the actual exhortation and, incidentally, showing that teaching the Dharma is the enlightened activity of the Buddha. ^1-7

###### Actual ^1-3-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-8]]

The actual exhortation. First, it is taught by a single verse starting with "Joy and respect...": Having 'brought close' the 'joy' that is mental clarity and aspiration towards the holy Dharma, 'and' the 'respect' with reverence for the one teaching the Dharma, 'and' the 'supreme faith' of conviction in the benefits of hearing the Great Vehicle and so forth; 'having dispelled' those things called 'afflictions', which are the five 'obscurations' such as sensual desire that act as hindrances to taking the Dharma to mind properly or retaining it; and 'passed beyond the stains' such as miserliness which are the factors discordant with the six perfections and act as hindrances to practicing or engaging in the Dharma; 'by that' noble person who has 'set out for' accomplishing the temporary and ultimate 'aims' of 'wanderers' through great compassion, it should be heard. 'Docile' (des pa) means having a gentle or peaceful nature; therefore, 'listen' to 'that' profound 'perfection of wisdom' which is the path of those great beings whose mind-streams have become docile through the union of the realization of emptiness and the mind of compassion, a path that is beyond the domain of śrāvakas and others, 'which' has 'become' the 'domain' of meditation and post-meditation of the heroic 'bodhisattvas'. In general, because the fruitions of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas also arise from some degree of realization of emptiness, such as realizing the selflessness of persons, when speaking generally of the greatness 'of' the qualities of realizing emptiness, it is taught to be the sole mother that gives birth to the four noble ones; and because the completely perfected perfection of wisdom that realizes all phenomena as equality is the uncommon cause of buddhahood itself, in that way one should understand many essential points, such as the ultimate establishment of the single vehicle. Incidentally, showing that teaching the Dharma is the enlightened activity of the Buddha. ^1-8

###### Incidentally Showing that Teaching the Dharma is the Buddha's Activity ^1-3-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-9]]

Second, there are three parts: the example, the meaning, and the reason, each in turn. ^1-9

###### Example ^1-3-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-10]]

They are taught by one verse each, starting with "In this Jambudvīpa...". If one thinks: "As just explained, if the perfection of wisdom to be heard is the uncommon domain of the heroic bodhisattvas, how can the elder Subhūti and others be able to teach it?" As an example: 'In this Jambudvīpa', 'whatever' 'rivers' like the Ganges 'flow' 'and' 'produce' whatever 'flowers' 'and' 'fruit-bearing' 'medicinal plants' 'and' 'forests' there are, 'that' 'is' the 'majestic power' of the previous aspirations 'of that' 'nāga lord', 'that' great 'abiding support', the 'nāga lord' devoid of the fears of hot sand and so forth, who has become the chief or 'king of nāgas' 'dwelling in' the great 'Anavatapta' lake. ^1-10

###### Meaning ^1-3-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-11]]

Like the example, 'whatever dharmas' 'the Victor's śrāvakas' such as Subhūti briefly 'teach', 'and' extensively 'explain', 'and' 'express' 'endowed with' the modes of the four kinds of 'reasoning' such as the reasoning of performing functions that establish them, 'and' which 'cause' the attainment of the ultimate fruition of teaching the Dharma, the 'supremely exalted' 'bliss' of non-abiding nirvāṇa, 'and' furthermore, 'obtaining' the four fruitions such as stream-enterer as temporary results—'all' 'those' 'results of that' teaching of the Dharma 'also' arise from the 'power of the person' of the 'Tathāgata'. ^1-11

###### Reason ^1-3-2-1-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-12]]

'If' one asks '"Why is that?"', 'whatever' 'system of Dharma' of selflessness 'the Victor' 'has taught', 'in that' the śrāvakas, 'who have become the disciples' of the 'supreme of humans', 'manifestly train'; 'and having directly' 'realized' it, 'they teach' to others also 'just as' they themselves 'have trained'. All 'that' is done 'by the power' of the 'Buddha'; 'it is not' 'by the power' of the śrāvakas' 'own' 'strength'. Furthermore, through the blessings of the Buddha, the sound of the profound Dharma emerges even from insentient space, from which disciples become fully liberated and so forth; thus, one should understand that the enlightened activity of the Buddha is inconceivable. The nature of the Dharma being taught. ^1-12

##### Nature of the Dharma Taught ^1-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-13]]

Second, the nature of the Dharma being taught. There are two parts: the path to be accomplished and the fruition of the path. ^1-13

###### Path to be Practised ^1-3-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-14]]

The path to be accomplished. For the first, there are two parts: explaining the perfection of wisdom, the principal of the path, and explaining the other five as companions. ^1-14

###### Explaining the Perfection of Wisdom, the Principal Path ^1-3-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-15]]

Explaining the perfection of wisdom, the principal of the path. For the first, there are three parts: the approach of teaching it briefly, the approach of teaching it at moderate length, and the approach of teaching it extensively. ^1-15

###### Gateway of Summarised Teaching ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-16]]

The approach of teaching it briefly. For the first, there are two parts: explaining extensively the support, the Dharma, and the fruition; and the concluding summary of those. ^1-16

###### Detailed Explanation of the Support, the Dharma, and the Fruit ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-17]]

Explaining extensively the support, the Dharma, and the fruition. For the first, there are three parts: the support, which is the person; the Dharma to be practiced; and teaching the fruition of that. The support, which is the person. ^1-17

###### The Person Who is the Support ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-18]]

For the first, there are three parts: the distinction of realization, the distinction of the spiritual friend, and the meaning of the word bodhisattva. ^1-18

###### Distinctive Feature of Realisation ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-19]]

The distinction of realization. For the first, there are three parts: the ground being objectless, the path being without conduct, and the mode of the fruition not abiding in the two extremes. ^1-19

###### Non-Apprehension of the Ground ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-20]]

The ground being objectless. For the first, there are three parts: teaching, explaining, and summarizing. From among those, briefly ^1-20

###### Teaching ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-21]]

teaching. For the first, the meaning of the single stanza 'In whichever wisdom' etc.: To whichever bodhisattva it is spoken thus: if the supreme perfection of wisdom to be taught or practiced is investigated by differentiating aspects of object and time—such as from what object it arises and to what person it belongs—it is not observed as established by its own intrinsic nature, but is established merely as posited by designations and conventions; and likewise, the object of the teaching or the practitioner, the bodhisattva, is also nothing more than a mere collection of the five aggregates, in which an intrinsic nature established as a person or phenomenon is not found in the slightest, and without being observed, remains as a mere conventional illusion; and the essence of that person practicing that path, which is called the ultimate mind of awakening naturally luminous—if that too is examined and investigated, because it does not abide in any extremes, it is not observed in the slightest as truly established in its own intrinsic nature. Having heard these words teaching the nature of the profound dharmadhātu which thus does not abide in any phenomena and transcends all spheres of observation, one is without delusion of grasping at true existence by thinking 'it is not so', and without being terrified at it being taught as emptiness. That bodhisattva who has comprehended the unity of the two truths—the inseparability of natural emptiness and appearance—possesses the fortune to engage in the profound wisdom realized and taught by the Sugata. Explaining extensively. ^1-21

###### Explanation ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-22]]

For the second, there are two parts: the object to be known not being observed, and the subject, wisdom, not being observed. ^1-22

###### Non-Apprehension of Objects of Knowledge ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-23]]

The object to be known not being observed. For the first, there are two parts: the meaning and the example. ^1-23

###### Meaning ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-24]]

For the first, the single stanza 'no form' etc.: Thus, if one investigates with the wisdom that investigates the ultimate, all this, however much appears as the five sense faculties and five sense objects included in the aggregate of form, is an accumulation and gathering of many particles; and in what is an accumulation of many, there is not the slightest truly established essence of a gross thing or a whole, and since by investigating whether even those subtle particles have directional parts or not, even the particles themselves are not truly established, one should know that this 'form' is of the nature of being 'without' truth even though it appears. Likewise, if one investigates the multiple and impermanent nature of all 'feelings' of pleasure, pain, and equanimity belonging to one's own or another's continuum, however many there are, not even a single very subtle part is truly established, 'and' so they are 'without' it; and similar to that, the aggregate of 'perception' and the aggregate of formations, that is, 'intention', are also 'without' being established by their own intrinsic nature in the slightest. 'In such a manner', what becomes a 'basis' or ground 'of consciousness' 'is not' existing even as a mere 'particle', because thus phenomena of mind and mental factors abide as being multiple, a true singularity is impossible in them, and when the earlier and later parts are investigated, an ultimately partless instant in time among things that are momentarily arising and ceasing is not established. Thus, since these five aggregates are merely appearances that are pleasant when unexamined, but when examined and investigated, they are not established as the essence of anything either subtle or gross, they are seen in the manner of a dream, an illusion, the moon in water, and so forth, appearing while not existing; and thus, all conditioned and unconditioned phenomena are not established in the slightest from their own side, but are mere appearances through the force of causes, conditions, and dependent origination; and when certainty arises that if this infallible appearance just as it appears is investigated by reasoning seeking suchness, it is found to be nothing more than posited by the intellect as self and various phenomena through the force of conception, without even a mere subtle particle being established as this or that, 'that' bodhisattva 'does not abide' in an objectifying manner in 'all' phenomena to be known either. Possessing an excellent view through the certainty of not abiding in any extreme, one acts without abiding in anything, or 'acts' 'without abiding'; and one should know the manner of all such statements as not seeing anything is taught to be perfect seeing, and similarly abiding in non-abiding, engaging in non-engaging, and so forth. Thus, this knowing of all phenomena of saṃsāra and nirvāṇa as lacking an established nature and being like an illusion is to be known as the certainty of post-meditation being like an illusion. This is the perfectly pure view possessing a mode of apprehension that is the object of the mind of the categorized ultimate. It is very important for beginners to become familiar and accustomed to this. For the authentic meaning of the ultimate abiding nature, all objects of the mind that follow words and concepts—such as 'lacking an established nature' and 'like an illusion'—are phenomena posited by the imputation of conception, and they too are conventional and fabricated, but they are not the essence of the ultimate, the unfabricated genuine character of dharmatā; therefore, the dharmadhātu in which all dualistic phenomena such as appearance and emptiness, and the conventional and the ultimate, become non-dual, the uncategorized ultimate to be realized by self-knowing pristine wisdom, transcending all spheres of words and concepts, which in the object of meditative equipoise appears like the center of space with an essence of equality where all phenomena are primordially without arising and ceasing, accepting and rejecting—that is the Great Mother of the victorious ones of the three times and their offspring, the abiding nature of the unarisen dharmadhātu, the naturally luminous essence of the ultimate mind of awakening, and the ultimate core of all phenomena; therefore, to enter into this way through a certainty free from doubt is absolutely crucial. As is also 'stated' in the Praise of the Mother by Rāhulabhadra: 'Beyond speech, thought, and expression is the perfection of wisdom. / Unarisen and unceasing, of the intrinsic nature of space, / The sphere of self-knowing pristine wisdom: / To the Mother of the victorious ones of the three times I pay homage.' And as stated in the Root Wisdom: 'Not known through another, peaceful, / Unelaborated by elaborations, / Without concepts, without multiplicity of meanings: / That is the characteristic of suchness.' Since that yogi resting in meditative equipoise on such profound dharmatā is 'without' 'completely grasping' at any phenomenon, all objectifications that are the sphere of consciousness and all networks of elaboration are thoroughly reversed; thus, in dependence on naturally luminous wisdom or self-arisen pristine wisdom lacking any aspect of a mode of apprehension and possessing the supreme of all aspects, one 'obtains' the great 'awakening' 'of the Sugatas'. Just as has been unpacked slightly extensively here, one should likewise understand whatever words refuting form and so forth occur in the contexts below. ^1-24

###### Example ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-25]]

Second, the example is taught in one verse, starting with "Just as a wanderer...": As an example, "just as" the non-Buddhist "wanderer" called Śreṇika (Bzo sbyangs) or known in some sources as Śreṇika (Phreng can), having seen with his own inquiring "knowledge" that no matter how much effort he made in the referential view asserting the existence of a self in his own tradition, it did not act as a cause for liberation from all bonds, came into the presence of the Teacher, the Bhagavan, and asked for the path to liberation; then, having gained exceptional conviction in the dharma of selflessness taught by the Teacher, he relied on the path of "non-referential" wisdom, such that the "destruction" of the "aggregates" of perpetuating suffering or the reversal of the continuum of saṃsāra "occurred" as nirvāṇa—"similarly, whatever bodhisattva knows dharmas" to be without referent "will not touch nirvāṇa" that is biased toward peace, and "that" bodhisattva "will abide" in the profound "wisdom" of the equality of existence and peace. Subjective wisdom being without referent. ^1-25

###### Non-Apprehension of the Subject, Wisdom ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-26]]

Second, there are three parts: teaching from the perspective of affirmation, teaching from the perspective of negation, and summarising their meaning. ^1-26

###### Teaching from the Perspective of Affirmation ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-27]]

First, this is taught in one verse starting with "Furthermore, this wisdom...": Thinking, "Furthermore, this" so-called "wisdom" that realises all dharmas to be without nature, being the sole source of all the qualities of nirvāṇa, "is" indeed to be sought after, nevertheless, when one examines and investigates, asking, "'What' is its nature? 'Whose is it'? 'From what' object 'is it'?", one knows that it too lacks any truly established singular nature; and, taking that as an illustration, one "examines" and investigates "all these dharmas" from form up to omniscience as being "empty" of their own nature. "Having closely examined" in this way with authentic reasoning, "that bodhisattva" who "does not shrink" back in bewilderment regarding the meaning of emptiness and "is unafraid" of the profound dharmatā "is close to" perfect "awakening". ^1-27

###### Teaching from the Perspective of Negation ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-28]]

Second, the meaning of the four lines starting with "If, without knowing...": "If, without knowing" the meaning of all dharmas being primordially empty, one abides in the "perception" that form is a fully established entity of "form," and likewise "acts" with the perception that clings to the reality of the "aggregates" of "feeling," "volition" (standing for formation), and "consciousness"; and if, by relying on rational analysis, one "conceives" that the nature of "these aggregates" is simply the non-affirming "emptiness" that negates the object of negation, true establishment, and that this alone is the natural state—"even if" one thinks so, "that bodhisattva," because they do not abide in the meaning of the natural state that is free from exaggeration and depreciation and is beyond the domain of conception, "is acting upon marks" involving reference, and "has not" obtained "faith" in the "unborn" profound "abode" of the dharmadhātu, which is destitute of all conceptual elaboration of marks; thus they should be understood as being bound by referential fixation and apprehension of marks. ^1-28

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-29]]

Summarising the meaning. ^1-29

###### Summary of Its Meaning ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-30]]

Third, the meaning of the four lines starting with "Whoever does not [apprehend] form...": The genuine meaning thus taught is this: "Whoever does not" apprehend "form" as form, and likewise "does not" apprehend it as "feeling," "does not" apprehend it even as "perception" and "volition," and "does not act" by apprehending "consciousness" as being established by its own nature; in short, who does not act upon referential marks such as existence and non-existence regarding any dharma such as the aggregates, and thus "acts without abiding" in anything—"that" person, no matter where they act, "does not refer to it as 'I act,'" because they have become "steadfast" in the markless "wisdom" that is free from all arrogance. Endowed with the "mind" that thus realises the "unborn," they "will touch" the "supremely peaceful" "samādhi" bearing names such as the hero's gait. The path lacking action. ^1-30

###### Non-Practice of the Path ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-31]]

Second, the path lacking action: The meaning of the two verses starting with "Whatever bodhisattva...": "Whatever bodhisattva", at "this" time of cultivating the path "like that", as just explained, "abides" with their mind "closely pacified"—"that" one "is prophesied" by "former" "tathāgatas" to unsurpassable perfect awakening. Such a bodhisattva "does not" generate the arrogant thought, "I rest in equipoise" upon emptiness, "or" "I have arisen" from it. "Why is that?" Because they "fully know" the "nature" of "all dharmas" to be non-dual dharmatā equality. "If they act like that", they "act" in the "wisdom" of the "sugatas"; because "that" bodhisattva "fully knows" that non-action regarding any dharma "is" the "supreme" action, "whatever dharmas" they "act" in, such as the path, "that" and the actor "will not be referred to". "Such" a manner "is acting" in the "supreme perfection of wisdom". The manner of the result not abiding in the two extremes. ^1-31

###### The Way the Result Does Not Abide in the Two Extremes ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-32]]

Third, there are two parts: the meaning and the example. ^1-32

###### Meaning ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-33]]

First, the meaning of the single verse starting with "Whoever [imagines] existence...": When it is taught that "whoever" [thinks] all these dharmas "are not existent" by their own nature, and whatever is designated as a dharma "is" "without nature"—having examined and investigated "that" meaning, "fools" who cling to names and marks "imagine" it as either the extreme of exaggeration of "existence" "or" the extreme of depreciation of "non-existence," but they are unable to comprehend the natural state free from extremes. In the authentic meaning, "both these" things called "existence and non-existence" "are" equally "dharmas without referent"; the meaning of the union that completely lacks abiding and is free from all conceptual elaborations exaggerated by fools, such as existence and non-existence, being and non-being, is the pacification of all aspects of extremes and assertions, to be individually known by oneself. If "whatever bodhisattva knows this" meaning of the dharmadhātu, which does not conceptualise existence and non-existence as two, they "will definitely emerge" from all extremes. ^1-33

###### Example ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-1-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-34]]

Second, the meaning of the single verse starting with "Whoever here [knows] the aggregates...": "Whoever", "here" during the occasion of training on the path, "knows" that "the" five "aggregates" and all dharmas they illustrate are, like the example, appearing yet untrue, "like an illusion," "and" furthermore "does not" treat "illusion as other and the aggregates as other," apprehending a difference in their degree of truth, but rather knows the five aggregates themselves as being equal to illusions and dreams; being "free from the manifold perceptions" of apprehending self and dharmas, "this action" in "closely pacified" conceptual elaboration "is action" in the "supreme perfection of wisdom." ^1-34

###### Attributes of the Spiritual Friend ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-35]]

Second, regarding the specific qualities of the spiritual friend, there are two parts: affirmation and negation. ^1-35

###### Affirmation ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-36]]

First, the meaning of the two lines starting with "[Endowed with] a spiritual friend...": Here, because bodhisattvas are endowed with outer and inner tutors, they will not fall away from the path of the supreme vehicle. If asked what [these are]: Through the outer tutor, wisdom, [they] show all dharmas to be without nature, and are "endowed with a spiritual friend" who shows the method of dedicating all roots of virtue into omniscient wisdom through skilful means; and endowed with the inner tutor, the mind desiring to attain omniscience for oneself, and "endowed with the superior seeing" that is pure in the three spheres—such a one upon "hearing" the profound perfection of wisdom, the generating "Mother of the victors," "will not be entirely terrified," but will become receptive to the meaning of the Mother. ^1-36

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-37]]

The specific qualities of the spiritual friend. ^1-37

###### Negation ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-38]]

Second, the meaning of the two lines starting with "Whoever...": Conversely to what was just explained, "whoever" as a beginner is "endowed with a sinful friend" who disparages the meaning of the supreme vehicle, and, because their own intellect is immature, is "reliant on others," meaning dependent on others, "that" one, for example, "like an unbaked vessel breaking as soon as it is touched by water," will fall away from the path of the supreme vehicle. The literal meaning of 'bodhisattva'. ^1-38

###### The Meaning of the Word Bodhisattva ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-39]]

Third, regarding the literal meaning of 'bodhisattva', there are two parts: the literal meaning of 'bodhisattva' and the literal meaning of 'great being'. ^1-39

###### The Meaning of the Term Bodhisattva ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-40]]

First, the meaning of the four lines starting with "Why is this...": "If" one asks, "'Why is' 'this' person to be designated 'a bodhisattva'?" Following the nature of the dharmadhātu, through the wisdom of hearing and so forth, they completely "cut off" all manifest adherence or "attachment" to all dharmas such as form, and completely abandoning conceptualisations of "attachment" through adhering as true to "all" knowable dharmas along with their seeds, they "desire" their "exhaustion"; and since they gradually "touch" the great "unattached" "awakening" "of the victors," "therefore" "this" person "obtains the name" "bodhisattva." ^1-40

###### The Meaning of the Term Great Bodhisattva ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-41]]

Second, there are two parts: becoming supreme among sentient beings, and the greatness of the armour. ^1-41

###### Becoming Supreme Among Sentient Beings ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-42]]

First, the meaning of the single verse starting with "Why is this...": "If" one also asks, "'Why is' 'this' one to be designated by the name 'mahāsattva'?" Because they generate the mind to fully protect all beings without exception and realise profound dharma, they "become the supreme and" pre-eminent "of the many" limitless, immeasurable "hosts of sentient beings" residing in the world systems, and since the endless "views" of the "realms of sentient beings", such as self and aggregates, are difficult to destroy due to being habituated since beginningless time, and are many because they are boundless, they are "great"; and yet all such views are "cut" and cleared away by this one who teaches the non-referential, profound dharma. "Therefore," they "are" "fully proclaimed" as "mahāsattva." ^1-42

###### Great Armour ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-43]]

Second, there are two parts: the actual [meaning] and the explanation of the manner of the armour. ^1-43

###### Actual ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-44]]

First, the meaning of the single verse starting with "Great giving and...": Not only what was just explained, but furthermore, because they don the great armour, they are also called 'mahāsattva'. If one asks what that armour is: having "great giving," without being attached to any possessions, is the armour of generosity; having a great volition or "great intellect" that abandons faulty discipline is the armour of discipline; because they are "endowed with" the armour of the other four, such as patience, they have "great power" to conquer their respective discordant classes, such as an agitated mind; and they possess "entry" into "the supreme" "great vehicle" "of the victors," engaging in the path along with its result from the generation of the mind up to omniscience. In short, by means of practicing the six perfections of the path, they "don the great armour" of irreversible courage towards all the dharmas of the path of the great vehicle which is boundless like space; and striving to dispel the thicket of views in all world systems of the ten directions, to liberate beings from lower realms, and so forth, "subduing" all "the magical displays of Māra," "therefore" they "are fully proclaimed as mahāsattva." ^1-44

###### Explaining the Manner of the Armour ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-45]]

Second, there are two parts: the example and the meaning. ^1-45

###### Example ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-46]]

First, the single verse starting with "For example, a magical illusion...": Here, the manner in which the bodhisattva dons the armour is of a fearless and unshrinking nature; why is that? It is like this: "for example," a "magician" creates a "magical emanation" of various forms at a "crossroads", and when demonstrating the illusion of "cutting" off "many tens of millions" of "heads" of "the majority of people", because "those to be killed" are mere magical emanations, however much they suffer and however many they are, he sees them as false; "just like that", the "bodhisattva" too, "fully knowing" "all these beings" whose suffering is vast and boundless to be similarly like "emanations" without true existence, "that" bodhisattva "has" not the slightest "fear" in taking on the burden of the welfare of boundless sentient beings until the very end of the future. ^1-46

###### Meaning ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-1-3-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-47]]

Second, the single verse starting with "Form and perception...": The reason why sentient beings are like magical emanations is this: what is called 'sentient being' is merely designated in dependence on just these five aggregates, but in reality, "form," "perception," "feeling," "volition," "and" "consciousness" are "not bound" by anyone and "not liberated" by anything; the reason for that is because these five aggregates "do not exist" ultimately. For that which has no inherent nature, there is no bondage or liberation, like space. Knowing it to be "like that", "this" "unshrinking mind" towards the manner of "engaging" in "accomplishing awakening" as a mere conventional designation is "the supreme armour" "of excellent beings." ^1-47

###### The Dharma to Be Practiced ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-48]]

The dharma to be practiced. Second, regarding the dharma to be practiced, there are two parts: briefly indicating the nature of the great vehicle, and explaining its nature in detail. ^1-48

###### Brief Explanation of the Essence of the Great Vehicle ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-49]]

Briefly indicating the nature of the great vehicle. First, there are two parts: conventionally explaining it through the literal meaning, nature, and function, and ultimately teaching them to be non-referential. ^1-49

###### Explanation Conventionally Through the Meaning of the Term, Essence, and Function ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-50]]

First, the meaning of the single verse starting with "For what reason is this...": "If one asks" "for what reason" "this" is designated as "the great vehicle" of the bodhisattva: because whatever person having "mounted" "that" great vehicle "turns away", that is, makes transcend sorrow, "all sentient beings" to be tamed, it is called a vehicle in the sense of a mount, because by this path oneself and all others are uplifted to the ultimate result. As for the nature of "this great vehicle", making room for immeasurable sentient beings, it possesses a profound nature that is "like space", the dharmadhātu which is the nature of the twenty emptinesses, and "like a great palace", it is endowed with immeasurable vast arrangements of samādhi, dhāraṇī, perfections, and so forth; and because its function upon engaging in such a manner is that it makes "the mind joyous" and the body "happy" temporarily with undefiled bliss for immeasurable sentient beings, and "manifestly showers down" the "bliss" of non-abiding nirvāṇa as the ultimate result, it is called "the supreme vehicle" or great vehicle. ^1-50

###### Teaching That Ultimately These Are Not Apprehended ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-51]]

Second, the meaning of the single verse starting with "Whoever, having mounted...": Ultimately, "whoever" is the person that "goes" "in the direction" of the result "having mounted" any great vehicle is "non-referential". If one asks, then, what is the meaning of "what is taught", that in dependence upon this vehicle one "will go" to "the transcendence of sorrow", the result? That is taught merely conventionally; in genuine reality, "going" to the transcendence of sorrow is "not referred to" or non-referential; "for example," when a "fire is extinguished" due to the exhaustion of firewood, "that" fire "has" absolutely "nowhere to go" in any other direction; "just like that," "by that cause" of the exhaustion of mistaken, deluded conceptualization, "that" person "is fully proclaimed as" "transcended from sorrow". ^1-51

###### Detailed Explanation of Its Nature ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-52]]

Explaining its nature in detail. Second, regarding the detailed explanation, there are two parts: teaching that the person and the path are without inherent nature, and summarizing its meaning. The person. ^1-52

###### Teaching That the Person and the Path Are Without Nature ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-53]]

First, there are two parts: teaching that the person accomplishing the path is non-referential, and teaching that the path of the perfection of wisdom is non-referential. ^1-53

###### Teaching That the Person Who Accomplishes the Path Is Not Apprehended ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-54]]

First, the meaning of the single verse starting with "The bodhisattva...": The "bodhisattva" who accomplishes the path is "not referred to" in any of these states: the "prior limit," "that is," the past time or the state of saṃsāra, "and" the "later limit," that is, the future time and things like the stage of no more learning, "and" "what occurs now," the time of training on the path; because the person is inherently unarisen from the beginning and does not arise later, they are "pure" of the characteristics of the "three times," "that is," they cannot be differentiated by any of them. "Whosoever" is called a bodhisattva, "they" are in reality "unconditioned," so they have "no" conventional "fabrications" positing them as a practitioner of the path, an attainer of the result, and so forth; knowing it to be like that, "this" "is" "practicing" "the supreme perfection of wisdom." Teaching that the path is without inherent nature. ^1-54

###### Teaching That the Path of the Perfection of Wisdom Is Not Apprehended ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-55]]

Second, there are two parts: what to engage in, and what to turn away from. ^1-55

###### What Is to Be Engaged In ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-56]]

First, the single verse starting with "A bodhisattva, wise and knowing...": At "the time" when "any" "bodhisattva" who is "wise" in the conventional and "knowing" the reality of the ultimate is practicing the conduct, having analyzed the nature of cause and result of all dharmas, they "thoroughly consider" the meaning of being "without arising" from the beginning, and "thus" they "practice" and abide. If one asks what that is, generating "great compassion" without reference, "yet" having "no perception" of "sentient beings", "this" "is" "practicing" "the supreme perfection of wisdom". ^1-56

###### What Is to Be Avoided ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-57]]

Second, the single verse starting with "If the perception of sentient beings...": "If" one has a true "perception" "of sentient beings," "and" similarly "generates" a "perception" "of suffering," "and" "thinks," "I shall perform the benefit of" "beings" and "abandon" their "suffering," the "bodhisattva" who "thoroughly conceptualizes" by way of clinging to "self" "and" "sentient beings" has not transcended all conceptualization; "someone like this" "is" "not" thoroughly "practicing" "the supreme perfection of wisdom," but abides on the level of having references. Summarizing its meaning. ^1-57

###### Summary of the Meaning ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-2-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-58]]

Third, the summary of the meaning. The single verse starting with "Just as oneself...": "Knowing" "all sentient beings" "as just like" "oneself," the bodhisattva, who is unestablished by inherent nature, and "knowing" "all dharmas" such as bodhicitta "as just like" "all sentient beings" who are without inherent nature, "this" one who abides in the unelaborated reality, the abiding nature of the inseparability of the two truths, "not conceptualizing" "either" "non-arising" "and" "arising," "is" "practicing" the state of "the supreme perfection of wisdom." Teaching its result. ^1-58

###### Teaching the Result ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-1-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-59]]

Third, the result. The meaning of the single verse starting with "The dharmas in the worlds...": If one asks what is to be attained by that non-dual non-conceptual wisdom? Having "abandoned" the extremes of ultimate "arising" "and" genuine "passing away" or ceasing "for all" those "as many as" can be "thoroughly expressed" by the "names" of "dharmas" "in the worlds," from form up to omniscience, and abiding in equality without arising and ceasing, one "attains" the Buddha's non-conceptual "wisdom," "which" never degenerates and thus is "immortal" and "than which" there is "no other" "supreme" thing, being the self-nature of the unsurpassable ultimate supreme of all. "Because of that," "this" path of realizing all dharmas as non-referential "is called" "the perfection of wisdom," the unsurpassable guru of all paths. ^1-59

###### Summary of Those Sections ^1-3-2-2-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-60]]

Summarizing the topic of these. Second, summarizing the topic. The single verse starting with "Whatever bodhisattva...": "Whatever" "bodhisattva," out of conviction in their own level, "practices" "thus" as taught, "without" "doubts" and hesitation, they are to be described as "possessing" the supreme "wisdom"; "that" one "is to be known" as "abiding" in the "equality" of all dharmas. Thus "thoroughly knowing" "all" "dharmas" of the ground, path, and result "as" of one taste in the equality of being "without inherent nature," "someone like this" "is" "practicing" "the supreme perfection of wisdom." The section on the medium teaching. ^1-60

###### The Approach of the Medium Teaching ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-61]]

Second, regarding the section of classifying it in a medium way, there are four parts: teaching the topic of how to train; identifying the perfection of wisdom itself which is the subject that trains in that way; generating the merit that depends on that; and the manner of generating its nature in one's continuum. From among these: ^1-61

###### Teaching the Topics of How to Train ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-62]]

Teaching the topic of how to train. First, there are two parts: the manner of not abiding, and explaining the manner of training in that. ^1-62

###### The Way of Non-Abiding ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-63]]

The manner of not abiding. First, there are two parts: explaining the abiding of not abiding, and its benefits. ^1-63

###### Explaining the Abiding of Non-Abiding ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-64]]

Explaining the abiding of not abiding. First, there are two parts: the meaning and the example. ^1-64

###### Meaning ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-65]]

First, there are two parts: not abiding in the bases of characteristics, form and so forth; and not abiding in the characteristics, impermanence and so forth. ^1-65

###### Non-Abiding in the Bases, Form and So Forth ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-66]]

First, the single verse starting with "Whoever does not abide in form...": "Whoever" "does not abide" "in form" in the manner of taking it as a reference point, "and" likewise "does not abide" "in feeling," "whoever" "does not abide" "in perception" "and" "does not abide" "in conditioning factors," "and whoever" "does not abide" "in whatever" "consciousness," they "abide" "in the true nature" of those, which is non-referential; "someone like this" "is" "practicing" "the supreme perfection of wisdom." ^1-66

###### Non-Abiding in Attributes, Impermanence and So Forth ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-67]]

Second, the single verse starting with "Permanent and impermanent...": Furthermore, they do not abide in any of the apprehensions of conceptuality or the two extremes regarding form and other dharmas, such as taking them to be "permanent" "and" "impermanent," "pleasant" "and" "painful," agreeable or "beautiful" and "ugly," "having a self" "and" "selfless," and grasping at them as "suchness" and grasping at them "likewise" as "empty"; "and" they "do not abide," with a perception that grasps at them as such, "in attaining" the three "results" such as stream-enterer, "and" "in" the "level" "of an arhat," "and" they "do not abide" in the "level" "of a pratyekabuddha" "and" "likewise" "in the level" "of a buddha"; in short, they do not abide through attachment to any of the dharmas of the ground, path, and result. ^1-67

###### Example ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-68]]

Second, the example. The single verse starting with "Just as the Guide...": As an example for not abiding in any dharma, "just as" the mind of "the Guide," the Buddha, the Bhagavan, "does not abide" solely in the "realm" "of the unconditioned," the peaceful expanse of nirvāṇa, "and" "likewise" "does not abide" "also in" "the conditioned," the side of saṃsāra of the three realms, but rather "practices" "without abiding" in any extreme of existence or peace; "likewise," "a bodhisattva" who "abides" "without abiding" through attachment "in any" "object" of knowledge "is" "abiding" "in whatever has no" designation as an "abiding place"; "the Victor," the king of Dharma, "taught" "that" "this" manner "is" "supreme" among all "abidings." ^1-68

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-69]]

Its ^1-69

###### Benefits ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-70]]

benefits. Second, the benefits. The single verse starting with "Those who... the Sugatas...": Thus, those who abide with manifest attachment in all dharmas of the aspects of the ground and path, and who possess referential marks, do not attain enlightenment; whereas by relying on the non-referential, one attains the three enlightenments: "Those who" "think" "to become" "śrāvakas" "of the Sugatas," "and" "those who wish" to become "pratyekabuddhas" "and" "likewise" "the king of Dharma," a buddha—in short, whichever of the three enlightenments one desires, "without relying" "on this" "path" "of patience" toward the meaning of non-abiding or non-referentiality, "one cannot" "attain" any of those results. "For example," "just as" "without relying on a boat," "one does not" "see" "the shores" for "going" to "this side" "and" "the other side" "of the ocean." To the extent that patience arises realizing the unapprehendable nature of the personal self and the phenomenal self, the three corresponding enlightenments will arise. By realizing personal selflessness, which is a particularity of emptiness, one attains the enlightenment of a śrāvaka; by realizing one and a half selflessnesses, one attains the enlightenment of a pratyekabuddha; and by realizing the ultimate equality where both selflessnesses are perfected, one attains the enlightenment of a buddha. Thus, all of these arise from the power of the perfection of wisdom. Therefore, it is taught that the extensive paths of the three vehicles arise from this perfection of wisdom. And as it is also taught in other sūtras, if a stream-enterer presumes, "I have attained the result of a stream-enterer," that itself becomes their grasping at a self of that, and so forth. Furthermore, regarding emptiness, the nature of the three doors of liberation, because śrāvakas lack skillful means and possess inferior intelligence, they momentarily do not realize it as it is due to their limited perception, and thus actualize the inferior nirvāṇa; but ultimately, having realized equality as it is, they become buddhas; therefore, it is taught that ultimately there is a single vehicle. And just as the Perfection of Wisdom sūtras themselves teach the distinction of the three—the knowledge of the bases, the knowledge of the path, and omniscient knowledge—and likewise as it is clearly explained in the treatise of the Regent, the tenth-bhūmi bodhisattva, generally, those with referential objects cannot be liberated from saṃsāra, and even for those who practice the non-referential perfection of wisdom, it is important to know that there is a difference of near and far from the resulting Mother depending on whether their realization is complete or incomplete. This was mentioned as an ancillary point. ^1-70

###### Explaining the Way of Training in That ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-71]]

Explaining how to train in that. Second, how to train in that has three parts: the basis of the training, the person who trains, and how to train. ^1-71

###### The Topics of Training ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-72]]

First, the single verse starting with "Teaching the Dharma...": Speaking from the perspective of the relative truth, a master "teaching" "the Dharma," "and" a student "listening," "and" "whatever" dharma "is" "to be explained," "and" "attaining" the four "results" of a śrāvaka by hearing the Dharma and practicing it earnestly, "and" likewise attaining the result of a "pratyekabuddha," "and" "likewise" whoever actualizes the enlightenment of a buddha, a "protector" "of the world," and "all" "those" ultimate "nirvāṇas" "attained" "by a clear-minded" one who is "wise" in the ways of the supramundane path—these mere appearances of dependent origination that are not established when analyzed by reasoning "were taught" "by the Tathāgata" "to be like" "an illusion." As it is stated in the extensive sūtras, Subhūti said: "O sons of gods, those who hear the Dharma from me should be sought as being like an illusion and a magical emanation," and up to, "If there were any other dharma superior to nirvāṇa, I would consider it too to be like an illusion." ^1-72

###### The Person Who Trains ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-73]]

Second, the single verse starting with "These four persons...": If one asks who reveres the meaning just explained: "These" "four persons" "are not terrified" "by that" profound meaning just explained. What are the four? "Sons of the Victor" who are "wise" "in the profound truth," meaning those who have seen the truth or purified their wisdom, "and" great bodhisattvas who are "irreversible," "and" noble "arhats"—those three, having "dispelled" "and" exhausted "the stains" of the mind, and having "abandoned doubt" regarding the profound meaning, are "fully perfected" in their intention. "And" the "fourth" is one who, although a beginner, because they have served the past Victors, is now "fully embraced" "by spiritual friends" capable of authentically teaching the perfection of wisdom. ^1-73

###### The Way of Training ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-74]]

Third, how to train has two parts: training in the manner of not training for the inferior results, and training in the manner of not training in all dharmas. ^1-74

###### Training in the Way of Not Training for the Inferior Fruit ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-75]]

First, it is taught by the single verse starting with "A bodhisattva who is wise and knows...": "A bodhisattva" who is "wise" regarding the states of the paths of the three vehicles and who "knows" the mode of the single vehicle, the equality of indivisible ultimate truth, and who "practices" "thus" with all dharmas having an equal taste free from extremes, "and" who "teaches" the nominal true nature without contradiction, "does not train" on the "level" that attains for oneself the state of an "arhat" śrāvaka, "and" "does not" train "on the level" of a "pratyekabuddha" who "realizes the conditions." Conventionally, what do they train in? They "train" "after" the "dharma" of the arrival and emergence of "buddhas," solely "for the sake" of "omniscience" itself. Even so, ultimately, because neither "whoever" "trains" nor its opposite, "does not train," are apprehended, "not training" in any dharma "is" "training" in the authentic path. ^1-75

###### Training in the Way of Not Training in All Phenomena ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-1-2-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-76]]

Second, the single verse starting with "Increase of form, fully...": One "does not train" "for the sake of" the "increase" "and" "full" "diminishment," that is, the decrease, "and" "full" "apprehension" of all dharmas subsumed by affliction and purification, "form" and so forth. Because all these dharmas are from the beginning unapprehendable and pure by nature, ultimately they never possess any increase or decrease, arising or ceasing, objects to be apprehended, and so forth. Having seen this natural innate meaning, by abiding in actionless equality, one "trains" by "not training" or not apprehending through the application "for the sake of" "fully" "apprehending" or accepting "various" "dharmas" with manifest attachment; "and" with their intention, they "fully" "hold" "to omniscience" in an indivisible manner; "and" through the power of the profound path of means and wisdom, they "definitely emerge" in that ultimate desired state. "Whoever" possesses these three qualities, "this" "is" the manner of "training" of bodhisattvas who "rejoice" "in the qualities" of the supreme vehicle. Recognizing the perfection of wisdom itself, the subject of such training. ^1-76

###### Identifying the Perfection of Wisdom as the Object of Training in That Way ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-77]]

Second, recognizing the perfection of wisdom, the subject of such training, has four parts: teaching it as free from being one and different, teaching it as infinite, teaching it as inconceivable, and teaching it as pure from the beginning. ^1-77

###### Teaching That It Is Free from Being One or Different ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-78]]

Teaching it as free from being one and different. First, the single verse starting with "Form is not wisdom...": If one asks what the so-called perfection of wisdom is like: "Form" "is" "not" the perfection of "wisdom," because the two are not one. Also, "wisdom" "does not" abide separately "in form," because it is not apprehended apart from it. "Likewise," "consciousness," "and" "perception," "and" "feeling," "and" "volition," or mental formations—"these" "are" "not" "wisdom," "and" "in these" "too" "wisdom" "is not" separate. The ground, the true nature of dharmas, the perfection of wisdom, cannot be expressed as either one with or different from form and so forth. Therefore, "this" dharmadhātu, the perfection of wisdom, is neither one with nor "different" from any phenomena, form and so forth, but because all dharmas are encompassed within its single nature, it is, for example, "similar" "to the element of space"; because it is differentiated merely as the dharmadhātu alone, there "is not" even a particle of dualistic dharma that is "different." Moreover, if the true nature or dharmadhātu existed separately from phenomena, there would be dharmas that are not its true nature and do not become the essence of the dharmadhātu, but that is impossible. If these phenomena, form and so forth, were one with the dharmadhātu, simply by seeing them one would see the truth, and there would be faults such as it being impossible to establish the relative and ultimate as separate. As it is also said: "The perfection of wisdom should be sought from the chapter of Subhūti." There, it says: "Kauśika, the perfection of wisdom should not be sought in form, nor should it be sought other than form, because those dharmas have a single characteristic, which is having no characteristic." One should know it as it is taught there. ^1-78

###### Teaching That It Is Limitless ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-79]]

Teaching it as infinite. Second, the single verse starting with "Of the apprehended...": "That" "nature" "of" "the apprehended," the objects to be apprehended, form and so forth, "is" "infinite" because it does not abide with boundaries such as "this" and "that"; "and" "whatever" "is" "that" "nature" "of beings" who conceptualize or apprehend them, it is "also" "infinite" through the essence of the dharmadhātu; "and" "that" "nature" "of the element of space," which serves as an example to illustrate the true nature, is "also" "infinite." "That" "wisdom" "of the Knower of the World," which realizes all those objects, is "also" "infinite" to that same extent. As it is said in the sūtra, "The perfection of wisdom is great, immeasurable, and infinite," and so forth. Because all dharmas have become the essence of the dharmadhātu, the dharmadhātu accommodates everything and has no apprehendable limit; and since the phenomena that are encompassed within its state also have no spatial boundaries or temporal limits, one should know it in this way as the nature of the great vast expanse of the true nature of equal taste. Teaching it as inconceivable. ^1-79

###### Teaching That It Is Inconceivable ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-80]]

Third, the single verse starting with "Perception is this side...": As long as there is "perception" that apprehends characteristics such as entity and non-entity, one does not go beyond the world, so the "Guide", the Sugata, "has fully proclaimed" it to "be" "this side," stating, "To whatever extent perception arises, to that extent there is saṃsāra." Therefore, through the wisdom of individual discrimination, having "dismantled" whatever is "perceived" and whatever has become an object of apprehension, "abandoning" all perception that apprehends characteristics "goes" to "the other side" of all the world. Thus, "those who" are "free from" all "perceptions" of apprehended objects, "having attained" such a true nature that is beyond the intellect, "have gone to the other side" of the path and "abide" "in the intent" of the definitive "words" "of the Teacher". Although all dharmas have by their own essence abided from the beginning in a nature that is unarisen and free from obscuration, through erroneous perception one imputes them as a self and various dharmas, and they are merely corrupted by conceptuality. One should know that the stopping of all perception by non-conceptual wisdom in accordance with the true nature, resting in meditative equipoise in the authentic limit, the dharmadhātu, is the ultimate perfection of wisdom that effortlessly maintains the genuine condition without removal or establishment. ^1-80

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-81]]

Teaching it as inconceivable. ^1-81

###### Teaching That It Is Primordially Pure ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-4-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-82]]

Fourth, there are two parts: the actual teaching that all dharmas illustrated by sentient beings are pure from the beginning, and the benefits of realizing it in this way. ^1-82

###### The Actual Primordial Purity of All Phenomena, Illustrated by Sentient Beings ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-4-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-83]]

First, the single verse starting with "If the Teacher...": "Even" "if" the authoritative "Teacher", the Sugata, "were to remain" "for aeons" "as numerous as the sands" of the river "Ganges", "and" "fully proclaim" the "sound" saying, "Sentient beings exist," since by their own essence they are unarisen "from the beginning" and abide in a "pure" nature, "how could" "sentient beings" exist or "arise"? Since the true nature of things is unchanging, no one can cause them to arise and so forth, therefore, if even the Teacher were to teach that they exist, they still would not be practiced as existent, so what need to speak of others? Seeing the meaning of being unarisen from the beginning in "this" way "is" "the practice" of the "supreme" "perfection" of "wisdom". Through these four verses recognizing the perfection of wisdom itself, one should establish the profound yoga of the authentic perfection of wisdom, which holds the manner of non-dual wisdom that is beyond the domain of consciousness. ^1-83

###### The Benefits of Realizing That ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-2-4-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-84]]

Second, the single verse starting with "When I...": "When" "I", the King of the Śākyas, "spoke" "in agreement" in all respects "with this" "supreme" "perfection of wisdom", having genuinely attained acceptance that dharmas are unarisen on the eighth bhūmi, "at that time", "the former" "Supreme of Beings" "prophesied" to "me", saying, "'You" "will become a Buddha" "in the future" "time'"; "thus" "the Victor" Śākyamuni himself "spoke". Previously, when our Teacher, having become the young brahmin boy Megha, scattered five utpala flowers to the Buddha Dīpaṃkara and laid out his golden-colored matted hair as a seat, from the mouth of that Buddha he was prophesied, "Brahmin boy, in future times, after countless aeons, in this very world system you will become a Tathāgata named Śākyamuni." Furthermore, at that time, having attained acceptance of non-arising, that brahmin boy was inseparable from the immeasurable dharmas of a Buddha through the manner of non-apprehending, and thus he attained the prophecy; it is the true nature that Buddhas prophesy those who attain the acceptance of non-arising. It is said that prior to that, for immeasurable aeons, having served countless Buddhas and generated the mind of omniscience, he had not attained a prophecy, because he had not previously attained such acceptance abiding in the perfection of wisdom in all respects. Amassing merit relying on that. ^1-84

###### Accumulating Merit Relying on That ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-85]]

Third, amassing merit relying on that has two parts: the benefits of holding it oneself and so forth, and the benefits of explaining it to others and so forth. ^1-85

###### The Benefits of Keeping It Oneself and So Forth ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-86]]

The benefits of holding it oneself and so forth. First, there are two parts: not being harmed, and gathering merit. ^1-86

###### Not Being Harmed ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-87]]

Not being harmed. First, the single verse starting with "The perfection of wisdom...": "Whoever," whether a son or daughter of noble family, having acted with "respect," "holds in mind" the text "and" "comprehends" the meaning of "this" "perfection" "of wisdom," "in which" the "Protector," the perfect Buddha along with his spiritual sons, "practices" through meditative equipoise and post-meditation, "that one" "will not be harmed" by the physical sufferings of "poison," "weapons," "fire," "and" "water," and furthermore will not be harmed by grasping which is like poison, aversion which is like weapons, attachment which is like fire, and ignorance which is like water. As for obstacles on the path, "the evil" "Māra" "and" "Māra's" "partisans," his dark-sided cohorts, "will not" "find" "even" "an opportunity," because they are protected by the power of the true nature, the Buddhas, the bodhisattvas, and the gods of the white side. Gathering merit. ^1-87

###### Gathering Merit ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-88]]

Second, there are two parts: the benefits of worshipping it, and the benefits of training in it. ^1-88

###### The Benefits of Offering ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-89]]

The benefits of worshipping it. First, the three verses starting with "From whatever Sugata": Regarding "whoever" constructs a stūpa enshrining the relics "from" "a Sugata" "who has completely passed beyond sorrow", because the object of connection is supremely exalted, the benefits are great; furthermore, regarding the particular substance of that "stūpa", if it is "made" "from" the "seven jewels" such as lapis lazuli, red pearl, musāragalva, aśmagarbha, crystal, gold, and silver, and one "worships" it; and furthermore, as for how many, as for the particular large number, if one constructs "such" "stūpas" of "the Sugatas" equal in number to "the sands" of the river "Ganges", "filling" "thousands of tens of millions" of Buddha "fields"; and if, as an example "taking up" a "full" "examination", as for the particular agents, "by all" "those" "sentient beings, however many" there "are" "in" "infinite" "tens of millions" of "fields", "doing" "no" "other" "work", with the particular objects of worship being "divine" "flowers", "supreme incense", "and" "unguents", and with the particular duration being "for an aeon or" "even more than that", if they were to "worship" in that manner uninterruptedly "three times" each day and night, "to that" there would indeed be immeasurable merit; but if "someone" else were to "write" "this" text, which is the non-dual "Mother" of the Sugatas teaching wisdom along with skillful means, "from which" cause "will arise" the unequalled qualities such as the "ten powers" of the "Guides", the Victors, "in a volume" "and hold" it in hand, "and" if they were to "pay homage" merely for a single occasion "with flowers" and "unguents", "that" "merit" of the former worshipping, having "made" such "stūpas", "would not amount to" even a hundred thousand ten-million trillionth "fraction", part, example, or cause of this latter one. ^1-89

###### The Benefits of Training ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-90]]

The benefits of training in it. Second, there are three parts: how by training in the Mother all the happiness of existence and peace arises, how by training in the Mother the worship of relics and so forth is acquired, and how by training in the Mother other roots of virtue become the transcendent perfection. ^1-90

###### How All the Happiness of Existence and Peace Arises from Training in the Mother ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-91]]

How by training in the Mother all the happiness of existence and peace arises. First, there are two parts: teaching the meaning, and stating an example for it. ^1-91

###### Teaching the Meaning ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-92]]

Teaching the meaning. First, there are two parts: the arising of the happiness of peace, and how the happiness of both existence and peace arises. ^1-92

###### The Arising of the Happiness of Peace ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-93]]

The arising of the happiness of peace. First, there are two parts: how the happiness of mere peace arises, and how the state of great peace, the Buddhas of the three times, arises. ^1-93

###### How the Happiness of Mere Peace Arises ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-1-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-94]]

How the happiness of mere peace arises. First, the two lines starting with "the wisdom of the Victors": "This" "perfection" of "wisdom" of the "Victors" is the "great" "knowledge mantra" or the unsurpassed knowledge mantra; regarding that, "knowledge mantra" means, from the sound of "mantra", to protect easily through knowledge and understanding, and among all such means of knowledge mantras, this one is great, because it permanently "pacifies" "all" "things" included within mental "sorrow" "and" physical "suffering" of "many", immeasurable "realms" of "sentient beings". It is what has "become" the "unsurpassed" "king". ^1-94

###### How the Buddhas of the Three Times Arise as the State of Great Peace ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-95]]

Second, the two lines starting with "Whoever benefits": "Whoever", namely the bodhisattvas "acting" in the "conduct" of Samantabhadra, "with" application that "benefits" "and" with the intention of "compassion" for all sentient beings, having "trained" "in this" great knowledge mantra, the perfection of wisdom, "those" "wise ones" will "attain" supreme, unsurpassed "awakening" in the future time, thus attaining Buddhahood. How the happiness of both existence and peace arises. ^1-95

###### How the Happiness of Both Existence and Peace Arises ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-96]]

Second, the two lines starting with "Conditioned happiness and": "Whatever is" the "conditioned" "happiness" of saṃsāra "and" the "unconditioned" "happiness" of complete liberation, "all" "that" "happiness" of saṃsāra and nirvāṇa "should be known" to "arise" "from this" perfection of wisdom, which is like the basis or essence of "all" "phenomena". Stating an example for it. ^1-96

###### Stating the Example ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-97]]

Second, regarding the example, there are two parts: how it produces all qualities using the example of the earth, and how all qualities follow after it using the example of a universal monarch. ^1-97

###### How All Qualities Are Generated through the Example of the Earth ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-98]]

First, the one stanza starting with "Having sown seeds": As for the example establishing it as the basis for all qualities of the path and the result, just as when "seeds" are "sown" and "rest" "in" the "earth", "they will arise" as shoots and so forth, and having "found" the "assemblage" of their respective causes and conditions, "many" "kinds" of "forms" "grow", "so too" "all" "those" "however many" "five" "perfections" of the path and "qualities" of "awakening" of the result "grow" "from" the "perfection" of "wisdom"; if generosity and so forth are embraced by the wisdom that does not conceptualize the three spheres, they become the genuine path, and it is established that they and the ultimate result have a relationship such that without the one, the other does not arise. Furthermore, from the wisdom that realizes suchness, omniscience is accomplished, and because that omniscience teaches all phenomena of cause and effect unmistakably, all roots of virtue will arise directly and indirectly. ^1-98

###### How All Qualities Follow This through the Example of the Universal Monarch ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-1-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-99]]

Second, the one stanza starting with "The universal monarch": Just as "from whatever" "path" "the universal" "monarch" "always" "travels", "from that very" "path" "all" the "seven" "precious" things of the kingdom "and" the fourfold "divisions" of the "army" travel, "so too" "from whatever" state of the mind-stream of a person and the path "this" "perfection" of "wisdom" of the "Victors" is present or "travels", "from that very" state, all the roots of virtue purified of the three spheres, which are the cause, and the phenomena of the result, that is, "all" the "phenomena" of the "qualities" of the path and the result, "will" "travel"; through realizing this, all white phenomena are gathered, increase, and will reach culmination. ^1-99

###### How Offerings Like Those for Relics Are Attained by Training in the Mother ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-100]]

How by training in the Mother the worship of relics and so forth is acquired. Second, regarding how by training in the Mother the worship of relics is acquired, there are two parts: the actual topic, and explaining its reason along with an example. ^1-100

###### The Actual ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-101]]

First, the one stanza starting with "The Victor to Śakra": When the "Victor" "thoroughly" "asked" a "question" to "Śakra", the latter "offered" this "reply": "If" there "were" "Buddha" "fields" equal in number to the "sand" of the "Ganges", and "all" "those" fields "were" "filled" by the "relics" of the "Victors" without any empty space, in the manner of being filled "to the brim", and one made two portions consisting of that and the perfection of wisdom written in a volume, and asked which of those one would choose and take, "I", Śakra, "would take" "this very" "perfection" of "wisdom" written in a volume. ^1-101

###### Explaining the Reason for That Along with an Example ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-2-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-102]]

Second, the three stanzas starting with "If you ask why": "If you ask" "why" one would set aside that many relics, which produce inexhaustible merit when offerings and so forth are made to them, and take this perfection of wisdom, "it is not that I" "lack devotion" "to" the "relics"; indeed I have devotion. "However", the reason for taking the perfection of wisdom is that since they are "thoroughly" "steeped" by such "wisdom", those relics "also" become worthy "of being worshipped". "For example", "just as" "people" "who rely on" the "king" "are respected" by others, the "relics" of the "Buddhas" possessing great qualities become like that "in dependence upon" the "perfection" of "wisdom". Well then, someone might ask: Since through the power of that perfection of wisdom the Buddha has passed into nirvāṇa and is currently devoid of activity, how are his relics worthy of worship? Just as a "precious" "jewel" that "possesses" "all" the "qualities" that grant desires and is "invaluable" "is placed" or put "in whatever" container, such as a "casket", that is, a box for hiding it, "that" container "is worthy of being paid homage" to in the world; and even "if" that jewel "is taken out" "from that" container, people "rejoice" "in" the "casket" used as the container where it was previously placed, thinking, 'This is the container where the precious jewel is placed, so it is unlike others'; and the acquisition of such "qualities" of that container "is" because "of that" "precious" "jewel". "Likewise", "through" the "qualities" of the supreme jewel of the "perfection" of "wisdom", "even though" the "Victor" "has passed into nirvāṇa", his "relics", which are like its container, "also" "acquire" "worship". "Therefore", because of that reason, "whoever", what person, desires to rejoice in and "uphold" the immeasurable "qualities" of the "Victors", "by them" "this" "perfection" of "wisdom" "should be taken" up; "this" "liberates" one from all bondage of marks and obscurations and "is" the "unsurpassed" essence of liberation. ^1-102

###### How by Training in the Mother Other Roots of Virtue Become Transcendent Perfections ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-3-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-103]]

How by training in the Mother other roots of virtue become the transcendent perfections. Third, regarding transforming other virtues into the transcendent path, there are two parts: making generosity and so forth into one mode, and teaching an example for it. ^1-103

###### Making Generosity and So Forth of One Form ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-3-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-104]]

First, the one stanza starting with "Generosity, generosity's": What "goes before" "generosity, of generosity", is "wisdom" - meaning it must be embraced by the wisdom that does not apprehend the three spheres, "and" "discipline", "patience", "diligence", and "meditative concentration" are "also" "likewise". Why is that? So that "all" those "phenomena" of "virtue", such as generosity, are "not wasted" as causes for saṃsāra and one-sided peace due to being tainted by apprehension, they are "completely" "held" within the state of the perfectly pure perfections; therefore, "this" wisdom "points out" the "very mode" of "all" "phenomena", such as generosity, having an equal taste in the nature of non-apprehension. ^1-104

###### Showing the Example for That ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-1-2-2-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-105]]

Second, the two stanzas starting with "For example, in Jambudvīpa": As for the mode of all other virtues such as generosity having a single taste in the nature of the perfection of wisdom, "if" one gives an "example", "in" "Jambudvīpa", even though those "many" "thousands" of "tens of millions" of different "trees", such as sandalwood and agalloch, have "various" "kinds" of colors, scents, and so forth, and "many" "forms" and "multiple" "kinds" of shapes of branches, trunks, flowers, fruits, and so forth, regarding their shadows, "apart from" "merely" "being spoken of" "in" the "categories" of "being" the "shadows" of the trees, as "shadows", they "do not have" "various" colors, scents, and so forth like the trees, "and" from the side of the shadow itself, they "do not" "exist" "as" the "specificities" of the individual trees. ^1-105

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-106]]

"Likewise", according to that example, "these" "five" "perfections", the paths "of the" "Victors", "also", if embraced by the wisdom that realizes suchness, "all" of them "will obtain" "the very" "name" "of the" "perfection" "of" "wisdom". Being counted among the supramundane perfections is by the power of wisdom, and all those paths become of one mode in the emptiness endowed with all supreme aspects. Not only at the time of the path, but if virtues "are" "completely" "dedicated" "for the sake of" "omniscience", like water that has reached the ocean, as the result of all those virtues, "all" "these" "six" "perfections" "become" "of one" "taste" "in" what is "called" perfect "awakening", and become spontaneously present as the dharmakāya of resultant pristine awareness, the emptiness endowed with all supreme aspects. ^1-106

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-107]]

The benefits of explaining it to others and so forth. ^1-107

###### The Benefits of Explaining to Others ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-2-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-108]]

Second, regarding the benefits of explaining it to others, there are two parts: the manner of explaining it, and the benefits of explaining it in that way. ^1-108

###### How to Explain It ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-2-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-109]]

The manner of explaining it. Regarding the first, there are two parts: the manner of explaining the fabricated perfection of wisdom, which is to be abandoned, and the manner of explaining the genuine perfection of wisdom, which is to be adopted. ^1-109

###### How to Explain the Counterfeit Perfection of Wisdom That Is to Be Abandoned ^1-3-2-2-1-1-2-3-2-1-1-0

![[bo-mipham-lekshe#^1-110]]
